ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-13.ana.ud1289e274	Honourable co-representatives! Former P.A. Thea Kweinen is dead, 85 years old. Thea Kweinden was born on 4 June 1930 in Harstad. After graduating artium in 1949 and the Secretary of Oslo School of Commerce in 1950, she worked a few years in bookstores before taking the bookstore school in 1953. After that, she worked for many years as a bookstore assistant and as a secretary in law stores. Thea Kübenn lived most of her life in Blackland, where she at the same time marked herself as a social enthusiast, among others. with involvement in the Sortland People's Library Collection and in the Local Red Cross Association. She also participated in local politics and represented the Right in Blackland Municipal Government and Presidency for several periods. For a long time she was a member of Nordland Højre's county government, and from 1975 to 1981 she was Vice President of the Norwegian National Broadcasting Board. Thea Kweinden was the first representative of Nordland in the 1981–84 ageing period and met regularly at Stortinget throughout the period. In the fall of 1985, she was elected as a permanent representative. She was re-elected in 1989, but chose to retire in 1993 to get more time for the family. At Stortinget, she was first a member of the Church and Education Committee and then of the Consumer and Administrative Committee. From 1989 to 1993, she was a member of the Social Committee. During this period she was also a member of the Right Group Board. Thea Kweinden was a member of the Norwegian Council of Stortinget's delegation throughout all his major years and actively participated in Nordic cooperation. She was primarily known as a social politician, but she also engaged in school policy and in a wide range of issues of interest to North Norway. For many years she was a driving force in the work of developing higher education in the northern part of the country. Thea Kwenjen enjoyed great respect in all political camps. She worked hard on the issues and was always open to listen to other people's arguments. She was wise, witty, kind, and caring. The Storting reminds Thea Kweinden of her work in Stortinget and shines peace over her memory. The representatives heard the President's commemorative speech.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-13.ana.ud1289e581	Today, we are considering the position of the Financial Committee on Prop. 100 S for 2015–2016, Prop. 101 S for 2015-2016 and Prop. 127 L for 2015-2016. These propositions concern the proposals made by the government on how the EU system of financial supervision and a selection of other acts can be incorporated into the EEA Agreement, that this proposal requires the transfer of powers to the EFTA Surveillance Authority and the EFTA Court in compliance with the Basic Law § 115, proposals for amendment to the EEA Agreement as a consequence, and proposals for a new law on EU financial supervision. First, I would like to thank the Committee for its good cooperation in setting up and handling the matter. There is a thorough explanation of the matter in the propositions. I will examine some aspects of the matter and the public debate on it, and present the majority position of the Labour Party, the Right, the Left and the Progress Party, and of the Labour Party. I expect the minority to explain their vision. The financial crisis in 2008 was international and revealed that many banks in a number of countries were not sufficiently sound. Many called for joint international regulation and supervision of banks and financial industries. In 2010, the EU Member States decided to set up a new common system of financial supervision, consisting of national financial supervision and four new institutions, in order to strengthen the soundness and consistency of regulatory application and supervision of the European financial industry. This is the European Banking Authority and the European Systemic Risk Board. [[ESMA]] [[ESRB]] Until 2010, the EU has cooperated on joint supervision between national financial regulators, where Norwegian financial regulators have also participated. It has also participated as an observer in the new financial supervision system from 2010. It has allowed the Norwegian Financial Supervisory Authority to influence and intervene in common legislation applicable to the European market, where Norwegian banks and financial institutions participate in the EEA Agreement on the same footing as European financial activities. In order for competition conditions to remain the same for all those involved in the internal market, including in the financial field, the EU requires the EEA countries Norway, Iceland and Liechtenstein to be subject to the same requirements and rules in the area of financial supervision as EU countries. The new EU system of financial supervision is therefore to be incorporated into the EEA Agreement. Negotiations have been in progress between the EU and EEA countries since the establishment of the Financial Supervision System, on how this can be done. The negotiations were concluded in October 2014 and the government proposes the negotiated solution. The Government proposes that Norway join the EU system of financial supervision through the existing two-pillar structure of the EEA Agreement and give the EFTA Surveillance Authority the responsibility for monitoring and action and the EFTA Court jurisdiction on the EEA/EFTA side. This includes, inter alia: that ESA can take binding decisions against the financial supervision and financial institutions of the EEA/EFTA countries and that the financial supervision of the EEA/EFTA countries remains fully involved in the supervisory authorities of the EU but without voting rights, as they have done informally since their establishment in 2011. The solution also means that ESA has the right to participate in EU supervisory authorities without the right to vote, and vice versa. The negotiated solution therefore implies that the Norwegian financial supervision is still to be overseen by the Norwegian financial institutions, while ESA monitors compliance with common rules on financial supervision, as ESA does in other areas covered by the EEA Agreement. In addition, ESA may take measures directly against financial institutions in certain areas and the EFTA Court is given jurisdiction. The majority of the Committee endorses the negotiated solution. Some counter-representations that have been promoted against the negotiated solution include: that the authority entrusted to the EFTA Surveillance Authority is only a sandeel. I will refer to the government's clarification in Chapter 9 of the Proposition. ‘The EFTA Surveillance Authority shall, when taking binding decisions, base its decision on a draft of the relevant EU supervisory authority. Such a draft is of a non-binding nature and does not constitute in itself an exercise of powers vis-à-vis private actors in the EEA/EFTA countries. The draft EU Supervisory Authority is also not legally binding on the EFTA Surveillance Authority». A point with a single market, which Norway is part of by virtue of the EEA Agreement, is that all participants experience a level playing field in that market. It is also a point that common conditions and rules are followed and that supervision and follow-up are also reasonably similar. It should therefore not be surprising that ESA should base itself on draft EU supervisory authorities when making decisions vis-à-vis financial institutions or financial supervision. Nevertheless, the fact that ESA is legally free is an important clarification. It is also claimed that Norwegian financial supervision cannot now adopt stricter rules for Norwegian banks and the Norwegian financial sector than the EU has. I'm not going to express myself on a general basis about how this will be, but as of today, it is at least so that they can. One thing that has been mentioned in particular: Today's economy on 4 April 2016, which concerns new capital requirements for Norwegian pension funds, shows that. Here, the Norwegian Financial Supervisory Authority has adopted stricter legislation for Norwegian pension funds than the common rules adopted in the EU by the new Financial Supervision System, by ensuring that pension funds and life insurance companies are treated in the same way with regard to soundness rules, which the pension funds, businesses and members believe can provide lower pensions to members and higher pension costs for businesses, according to the paper. This shows that at least the way it works today, it can do so, and I have no reason to believe that it will be different in the future, provided that there are minimum rules. It is also argued that Norwegian financial institutions have not encountered any problems on the European market since the establishment of their financial supervision by the EU and therefore there is no urgency in clarifying it. Financial supervision in the EU entered into force in 2010/2011. Under the EEA Agreement, new legislation shall be incorporated into the EEA Agreement no later than six months after its entry into force in the EU. Negotiations have been in progress since 2010. According to the proposal, a large number of acts are not incorporated into the EEA Agreement pending a solution. This means that there is a growing gap between obligations and rights between EU and EEA countries. Where new legislation is not implemented in the EEA Agreement, there may subsequently be questions as to how to proceed with the EEA Agreement and as stated in the proposal: ‘Without the inclusion in the EEA Agreement of the acts outstanding, it will in the long term be difficult to maintain functioning EEA cooperation for financial services.’ This creates uncertainty about the future of the EEA Agreement. We don't think that's what we're doing. Trade is important for Norway and the Norwegian economy. Trade creates jobs, which is the basis for us being able to build up the welfare state that we know today. Over 80 pst. Norway’s exports go to EU countries. For the Workers' Party, it is important to ensure the EEA Agreement in further cooperation with the EU and to contribute to the continued access of the financial industry and other industries to the European market on an equal footing with European industries. It is important for industry, and it is important for Norway. The working party believes that this solution ensures it. So the relationship to the Constitution. The proposal involves the transfer of monitoring and action powers to the EFTA Surveillance Authority ESA and jurisdiction to the EFTA Court. This transfer of authority is considered by the government to be more than negligible, and therefore consent of the Storting is requested in accordance with the Basic Law § 115. Paragraph 115 of the Constitution, before § 93, was previously used once, when the EEA Agreement was concluded in 1992 and competence was transferred to ESA and the EFTA Court in the field of competition. It is now proposed that powers be transferred to the same institutions in the field of financial supervision. The constitutional side of the case is discussed in detail in Prop. 100 S, where assessments of the transfer of powers to ESA and the EFTA Court of Justice by the Legal Service have been submitted in connection with the participation in the European System of Financial Supervisors. As is known, the Legal Service provides its legal professional judgements as an expert body independent of political leadership. To this end, the procedure has been applied in accordance with the second paragraph of Section 26 of the Basic Law in all cases where the consent of the Stortinge has been obtained for Norway's adoption of EEA Committee decisions incorporating new legal acts with adaptations to the EEA Agreement involving transfer of powers, as the threshold of under-interventionary transfer of authority has not been considered to be exceeded. That's what Prop shows. 100 S that the Ministry of Finance has obtained a total of four assessments of the constitutional conditions of the various models that have emerged in the negotiations on the EEA adaptation, on which they have been submitted. The law department's assessments are therefore that parts of the transfer of authority in the financial field are more than minor and therefore recommend that the matter be dealt with in accordance with the Basic Law, § 115, which requires a majority of three-quarters. However, it is the Storting itself that assesses whether this is in line with the provisions of the Constitution and what paragraph the case should be dealt with after. But there is a long and secure tradition that the Storting builds its assessments on the basis of which the government provides, and the government is constitutionally responsible for the accuracy and coverage of the information provided. The institutional aspects of this matter have also been part of the proceedings of the Financial Committee and of the Foreign Affairs Committee. The Financial Committee had an in-depth consultation on the matter, where these issues were also well-explained. The majority support the government's assessment. Finally, some believe that Storting should take longer to deal with this matter. We were also in the pre-treatment area today. Why the matter was submitted by the government for treatment in Stortinget so late in the spring session, the government itself has to answer. However, the matter was sent to Stortinget within the time limit set by Stortinget, on 15 April, so that the government can wait for the matter to be dealt with in the spring session. If the Committee were to spend a longer period of time, it was an assessment matter, but it was a unanimous committee, with the exception of the Centre Party, which decided to examine it now. There is also no new and unknown matter. It has been known and discussed since the establishment of the European System of Financial Supervision in 2010, when it was considered to be EEA-relevant. Negotiations between the EEA countries and the EU have been ongoing for the same period, and the Storting has been regularly informed about progress, through financial market notifications, state budgets, statements by the Minister of Foreign Affairs on the EU/EEA and in the meetings of the European Commission, both the previous and the current government. At the meeting of the European Committee on 13 October 2014, the Minister of Finance and the Minister of Europe reviewed the main features of the solution we are discussing here today, that it is within the two-pillar system of the EEA Agreement, and that it involves the transfer of powers in some areas to ESA and the EFTA Court, which require the consent of the United Kingdom by a three-quarters majority, as stated. The Constitution § 115. The size has also been continuously informed about the matter, which started under the previous government, which has been important to us in the treatment of it. It is underlined in the proposition that the government does not want Norway to delay the entry into force of the Regulations and has therefore decided to seek the consent of the Storting before the decision is taken by the EEA Committee. The financial industry also stressed in the hearing that it is important to consider the matter now in order not to delay its entry into force. The Labour Party is pleased that we have agreed with the EU and the other EEA countries on a proposal for the implementation of the financial supervision system in the internal market and is in favour of the negotiated solution. With this I present the position of the Financial Committee in the matter.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-13.ana.ud1289e688	‘The financial crisis clearly showed how disastrous the consequences can be for a country if there is no control of the financial market. In a number of European countries, hundreds of thousands of people experienced job losses. Fortunately, Norway managed better through the latest financial crisis than most EU countries. In Norway, we learned from the experience of the banking crisis 25 years ago. We know how important it is with good supervision and supervision of the banks in the country. This was highlighted by the Financial Crisis Committee, which showed the importance of Norway’s choice to operate with stricter rules than the EU system.’ So begins a chronicle written by the representative Trygve Schlagsvold Vedum, published at the NRK Opinion yesterday. And that's how far I agree. Therefore, Norway also has stricter capital requirements and regulations than many locations in the EU. We will also continue to do so as we strengthen the supervision of financial markets through a link with the EU financial supervision. Thus, the Norwegian Financial Supervisory Authority is not to be placed under the different financial supervision of the EU, but to be linked to supervisory practices that allow the control and supervision of a cross-border financial market, where Norwegian authorities may fall short. The alarming fact is that when the financial crisis developed in Europe, two of the three parties in government are now in favour of better supervision, transparency and control of financial markets. I had actually thought that it would make a little more impression to have to provide bank guarantees for 350 billion. kr through government bonds and a crisis package of 25 billion. kr, but obviously not. Despite Norway's good control and supervision of the banks, the financial crisis flourished in our markets, precisely because the financial markets are international. That is why we want a link with the European supervisory authorities. Norwegian securities are currently traded at European-wide marketplaces, even outside the control of Norwegian supervisory authorities. Norwegian financial corporations are customers of and suppliers of international capital markets. New EU regulations and supervision will thus safeguard Norwegian interests in Europe when Norway itself does not have the power to do so. But of course, it requires that we participate in the cooperation on enhanced supervision in our most important market. A Norwegian financial market does not function well without good and continuous access to the international capital market. At present, more than 180 legal acts are pending in Norway pending a solution for international supervisory cooperation. For each year there has been a greater gap between the commitments and the rights of the EU and EFTA countries. If this does not take place, we will lose a homogeneous internal market in the EEA for financial services. A no to well-functioning international regulation and supervision of the financial industry does not serve Norwegian interests. I would like to take a few examples of issues where the link to EU financial supervision will play a role. There is regulation of credit rating agencies, OTC derivatives and short selling. All three were assessed as central to the development of the financial crisis. The credit rating agencies were considered to have contributed to the crisis by underestimating the credit risk of structured credit instruments and by not adjusting the assessments when the market changed. Therefore, a detailed regulation is now in place to ensure the independence of credit rating agencies, improve the quality of credit ratings, increase transparency and provide an effective framework for registration and supervision. Supervision has been added to the European Securities and Markets Authority, ESMA. I'm in favour of this control. This is the Center Party and the SV against. I would encourage them to make a better proposal for the regulation and control of credit rating agencies. Before the financial crisis, Lehman Brothers and AIG were two of the largest derivatives trading actors. One went as a known bankruptcy, the other had to be saved by the taxpayers. Therefore, following the financial crisis, there was a broad consensus that the huge market for derivatives had to be better regulated, and already at the G20 meeting in 2009, Member States agreed that trading in OTC, or ‘over the counter’ derivatives, would go through a central counterparty that would be subject to both reporting obligations and capital requirements. The new regulations have contributed to both a reduction in systemic risk and a reduction in the risk of a party failing to meet its obligations. For Norway, this means an obligation to report OTC derivative contracts to a registered trade repository and an obligation to establish risk mitigation measures against OTC derivative contracts not managed by a CCP. This monitoring is carried out in cooperation with national authorities and the European Union’s oversight. We want to participate in supervisory cooperation because the financial market is international, and it is not only useful to have Norwegian financial supervision on one side of the transaction if it cannot be fully followed through. So the right wants more control over the international financial market than SV. My last example is short sales, which usually means the sale of a financial instrument that the seller does not own at the time the sale agreement is concluded. Short sales may help balance the market and mitigate the consequences of one-sided information. But during the financial crisis, trust between the banks and the banks was gone. The fear was great that the banks were unable to finance themselves, and many feared a so-called ‘run’, where depositors demand their money back immediately. In such a stressed market, short selling can seem very negative, and huge values can be erased in a short time due to market panic. In 2008, therefore, several countries introduced measures which varied in scope or prohibited the short sale of certain or all securities. The measures in the different countries were taken independently of each other based on national legislation. Experience from the financial crisis showed that these uncoordinated national measures were ineffective. This experience led to the introduction of uniform reporting obligations for a number of financial instruments through the Short Sale Regulation. The goal is, among others: increased transparency and reduced risk. National authorities will retain the possibility to prohibit or restrict certain types of short selling or similar other lawful transactions. However, in such cases it shall also ensure cooperation, on the one hand between national supervisory authorities and, on the other hand, between national authorities and ESMA. In particular, this Regulation is opposed to the Central Party, since, in a crisis situation, the EU supervisory authorities may intervene with the authorities of EU countries if they do not take action in a stressed market where large amounts may be lost. When we see how the financial crisis grew, also to countries with a stable and good economy, such as Norway in 2009, it is quite incomprehensible that someone can be against this. Only the crudest speculators in the financial market will benefit if Norway is outside this cooperation. So, of all the predators that the Center Party has chosen to protect, they choose international financial sharks. The EU wants sound and stable financial institutions and has put in place harmonised and strict regulation of financial markets to prevent the recurrence of the financial crisis. However, in order to ensure uniform application of the rules across the European market, there is a common European supervisory practice in place that oversees the supervisory authorities. It is related to this supervision we are now discussing in Stortinget. So this is not about for or against the EU, for or against the euro, Schengen or Norwegian border control. It is about the soundness of regulation and supervision of a cross-border financial market, where experience from the financial crisis shows that coordinated action, regulation and follow-up are necessary. The ESAs are not just a cornerstone of the EU’s response to the financial crisis. Strong and coordinated financial supervision is essential to preserve and further develop a single market for financial services. I have registered that someone thinks it is not urgent, but the work on finding a solution started six years ago, and for every single year, the number of acts has increased and thus spread between the commitments and the rights of the EU and EFTA countries. If this does not happen, we will lose a homogeneous internal market in the EEA for financial services. It's not right that this should be dealt with urgently. The case has been discussed and addressed in the Stortinget in all financial reports since 2009 and in the State budget in 2010, 2011, 2014, 2015 and 2016. It may be more important that the matter has been included in the presentations on important EU and EEA matters every year since this government joined, where the principles of the solution we are discussing today were presented as early as November 2014, with broad support at the time. From my knowledge to the opposition in this room, both the constructive and the other, I would think that this was something you wanted more of, not less of. This concerns the soundness of the regulation and supervision of a cross-border financial market, where experience shows that coordinated action, regulation and cross-border follow-up are necessary. It is only then to thank the rapporteur for a very good and thorough work on the matter and also the committee for the discussion so far.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-13.ana.ud1289e771	The EEA Agreement was ratified by Stortinget on 16 October 1992. After two days of intense debate and strong contradictions, it became an overwhelming majority to link Norway to the single market in Europe. There was a far greater transfer of competence to the EFTA bodies than the realities of the issues currently being dealt with by the Storting on common rules for the financial market in Europe. Until recently, proposals for new investigations and assessments were also submitted at the time, but the Stortinget concluded that all aspects of the EEA Agreement had been thoroughly examined in order to take the decision to safeguard Norwegian interests linked to the European Community, which was also in strong development. Some claimed at the time that the Storting said yes to the ‘goods and services’ Europe by accepting the four freedoms as an absolute prerequisite for European economic cooperation. In 1994, Norway actually said no to political Europe, and a majority of the people did not want Norway to become a member of the EU. Now Norway has more than 20 years of experience with the EEA Agreement, and experience suggests that this agreement has served Norway’s interests very well. It currently ensures Norwegian enterprises and consumers access to a market many times larger than in 1992 — now with more than half a billion inhabitants. Uncertainty about the future and development of the EEA was prominent in the debate almost 24 years ago because the agreement could not be static but on the contrary provided for a dynamic adaptation to the development of new EU legislation, and not least Norway could be confronted with EU requirements that would not be fully compatible with its own Norwegian interests at all times. The challenges have arisen, and they have been solved continuously through political processes and influence for the benefit of Norway. The EEA Agreement has clauses allowing new areas to be included in the agreement, just as areas can be excluded. The arrangement provides for adaptation to the legal obligations applicable to the EEA/EFTA countries and procedures are in place to address such issues. The EEA Agreement has ensured Norwegian industry and Norwegian industry in general predictability and non-discrimination in the European market. In 1992 the Stortingen decided to give sovereignty to the EFTA Surveillance Authority ESA and the EFTA Court in order to ensure the supervision and control of competition rules, public tender rules and State aid rules. ESA was given the opportunity to exercise direct authority over private legal entities in Norway in order to meet the requirements of free competition both for Norwegian and foreign actors – of course a major challenge for those seeking protectionism and regulation rather than free and dynamic competition on equal terms. The issues raised by Stortinget today do not concern EU membership or the transfer of Norwegian financial supervision to the EU, as attempted in less nuanced opinions on the network and in the media. Nor does it apply to or contrary to the EEA Agreement, even if a no can become a serious challenge to the agreement. It is essentially about how Norway should comply with its EEA obligations under the principles of cooperation. It is a complex and comprehensive area we are dealing with now, and I have great respect for the strong commitment in many environments, including those who are very dependent on Norway joining a common regulatory framework for the financial market in Europe. Both Finans Norway and the Finansförbundet have clearly expressed support for the solution negotiated by the government, while at the same time referring to the unfortunate aspects by saying no to common legislation. There is reason to stress that no-one claims that the internal market is closed for Norwegian financial institutions with immediate effect if we do not adhere to this legislation. However, 180 new legal acts are waiting for implementation in the EEA Agreement. If there is no solution to the issue of financial supervision, so that these acts can be incorporated into the EEA Agreement, we may risk gradually putting it out of play in the financial field. Norwegian financial institutions will benefit from continued access to the European market, and Norwegian consumers will benefit from Norwegian financial institutions being exposed to competition in Norway. Norway’s accession to the EU’s new financial market supervisory body has a long history, and it has been regularly reported back to the Stortingen on the negotiations with the EU. The process actually started in 2009 and there has been an expressed wish from Norwegian point of view that it would be appropriate to establish an adaptation in line with the two-pillar structure between the EU and the EFTA/EEA countries. Direct membership and voting rights of the EU Supervisory Body have been rejected by the EU because we are not a member. The government’s proposal does not imply the surrender of powers to the EU but the surrender of limited administrative and decision-making powers to EFTA bodies, where Norway is a dominant member. The decision-making competence is secondary and can only be used under certain conditions and in specific situations where national supervisory authorities have failed to fulfil their obligations under the supervision regulation. The draft decisions of the EU Supervisory Body shall be formally dealt with and adopted by ESA, but ESA is not legally obliged to accede to any decision. Disagreements need to be discussed in the EEA Committee between representatives of the EFTA countries and the European Commission, and decisions are anchored in agreement within the EEA Committee. It is therefore the ESA that is to take the formal decisions addressed to national authorities and to EEA/EFTA undertakings, based on drafts from the relevant supervisory authorities of the EU, and Norway is involved in their decision-making and preparatory work. It will continue to be the Norwegian Financial Supervisory Authority that supervises and controls Norwegian financial institutions and the Norwegian market, but in some demarcated areas ESA will be empowered to take decisions with direct consistency for Norwegian actors. The opposition to the EEA Agreement, and thus against the government's proposal to join the common financial market rules, pushes many lawyers and professors to their breasts. Of course, we need to listen to the expertise, but there is also considerable expertise – not least legal – that has designed the solution that is now recommended by the majority of the Committee. Professor Eivind Smith said to the Nations on 24 May this year that the government's proposal for treatment under the Constitution § 115 is a « bypassing the requirements of Grundlova». The same professor has characterised the EEA Agreement as a ‘constitutional disaster’, despite the fact that the Stortinge has ratified the agreement by a three-quarters majority. The Progress Party is in favour of the EEA Agreement, and we are therefore in favour of the solution that is now available from the Government. Cross-border supervision and control of financial markets are important and there is a need to ensure the same level of regulatory practice in order to ensure financial stability, protect Norwegian consumers and continue access to the European market for Norwegian financial industries. Economics are becoming increasingly global. With that, financial institutions must also become more global. This also allows problems in a financial company or financial market to have negative consequences in many countries. There is therefore a need for cross-border cooperation on regulation of the sector – it showed the financial crisis. Lack of international cooperation can easily mean poor or no regulation of the sector, and then we know from experience that the bill can easily be sent to taxpayers, including in Norway.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-13.ana.ud1289e845	The financial crisis hit the EU hard. The experience in many EU countries was that the regulation and supervision of banks and financial institutions were too weak. Therefore, in the wake of the crisis, the EU has proposed stricter financial market regulation and a new common financial supervision. Many of the new regulations are EEA-relevant and have been introduced in Norway, largely without challenges for our part. However, Norway’s role in EU financial supervision has been a demanding matter for the Norwegian authorities, and it has been a demanding matter for this government, as it was for the previous government. For five years Norway has been negotiating with the EU. The reason why the negotiations have taken so long is, of course, that the EU has been very reluctant to give what we believe to have been right, from Norway’s point of view. Getting the full vote on the new financial supervision was not realistic since we are not a member of the EU. However, we have fought for a plan B which would imply voting rights for the financial supervision of EFTA States in matters affecting the authorities and market actors of the EFTA States. It has been important from the Christian People's Party to seek a solution along that very axis – both because it would have maintained a real influence far more than what we are now facing, and because it would have been much more in line with the Constitution § 115, which requires Norwegian participation in international bodies to which we give sovereignty. It would have ensured Norwegian influence over decisions in the EU’s financial supervision affecting Norway and thus greater opportunities for more accurate and better decisions adapted to the situation in Norway and I would say our strict and successful supervisory tradition here in the country. To date, this has not been possible for the EU to participate. The result is a solution that in some areas places Norwegian financial supervision under EU supervisory bodies, practically without Norwegian voting rights. A design has been made in which all decisions in Norwegian cases will be formally taken by the EFTA Surveillance Authority ESA, but in practice the ESA will have no room for action relevant to taking decisions other than those of the EU Financial Supervision. The proposal therefore also requires the agreement of three-quarters of the representatives of the Storting. In this way, I commend the government for the procedure you have chosen constitutionally. This argues, in our opinion, that the proposed solution is not accepted. There is great reason to believe that this decision today will create a precedent for other supervision. EU financial supervision is part of a general trend in the EU towards increased use of supervision in both the further development and enforcement of rules in the internal market. These inspections are increasingly given the competence to take decisions that are binding on the authorities and businesses of the Member States. The manner in which Norwegian sovereignty waivers are made in this case, i.e. no Norwegian voting rights in matters relating to Norway, will very easily become the EU’s position in a number of future cases. There are other EU supervision on the steps in terms of both energy, telecommunications and data supervision. This has also been confirmed by the government. ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-13.ana.ud1289e1179	The work on finding a solution to the relationship between the EFTA States and the European System of Financial Supervision was initiated by Minister of Finance Sigbjørn Johnsen as early as 2009. In October 2014, I agreed with the Ministers for Economic Affairs and Finance of the EU Member States and Iceland and Liechtenstein on the principles of a link. The relationship is, in short and overall terms, based on the two main elements of the EEA Agreement, which are consistent regulation in two separate pillars. I briefed the Storting European Committee on these principles on 13 October 2014. The EEA Minister and EU Minister Helgesen briefed the Storting on these principles in its statement on EEA cases on 20 November 2014. After this, Stortinget has been regularly informed of the work on concrete adaptation texts that operationalise these principles. Work started until March this year. The proposal submitted on 15 April contains draft decisions in the EEA Committee with concrete adaptation texts in line with the principles agreed in October 2014. The present draft EEA Committee decisions require a fully demarcated authority to be transferred from the Financial Supervisory Authority to the EFTA Surveillance Authority. The supervision of Norwegian financial undertakings and the Norwegian financial market shall continue to be carried out by the Financial Supervisory Authority, with the exception of the supervision of credit rating agencies and some types of securities records. The EFTA Surveillance Authority is further empowered to: prohibit or restrict certain types of financial activities or savings products where this is not adequately managed by national supervision to resolve disagreements between national supervisory authorities and to take decisions directly against market participants where national supervision would not do its job; As noted above, all the materiality of the supervision of the financial market and the financial undertakings in Norway shall remain with the Norwegian Financial Supervisory Authority. Since this transfer of powers to the EFTA Surveillance Authority in the sense of the Basic Law is considered to be ‘more than negligible’, we ask that the Stortinget consent by a majority of three-quarters, as stated above. The Constitution § 115. First, what we achieve with these agreements is a formal basis for the Financial Supervisory Authority to participate in the cooperation between national supervisory authorities in charge of the European System of Financial Supervisors. A formal link between the Norwegian Financial Supervisory Authority of the EU Financial Supervisory Authority, where the Financial Supervisory Authority, with the exception of the voting rights, will participate fully, will allow the Financial Supervisory Authority to exercise influence over the professional assessments carried out by the supervisory authorities. Financial supervision may participate in different preparatory bodies and sub-groups, in line with EU countries, have the right to speak in the decision-making body and to be a member of colleges of supervisors of financial institutions with cross-border activities relevant to Norway. Secondly, I would like to point out that the main purpose of the EEA Agreement is the participation of EFTA States in the internal market for, inter alia, the following: goods and services and shall comply with an EEA legislation corresponding to EU legislation. This basic mechanism does not change through the link with the EU system of financial supervision. The financial crisis in 2008-2009 showed that a single European regulatory framework is necessary to avoid new crises in the banking sector. A crisis in the banking sector in one country will affect several countries, which requires international cooperation. Following the financial crisis, the EU has developed a great deal of new legislation on the financial market. Many of these EU acts contain references to the EU financial supervisory authorities. Therefore, in order to incorporate such legislation into the EEA Agreement, we need to have a solution for the relationship between the EFTA States and the European System of Financial Supervisors. Today we have 180 legal acts that have not been incorporated into the EEA Agreement, pending the issue of Stortinget. We have in Norway, for example. already introduced new capital requirements for banks, which were adopted in the spring of 2013, and for insurance companies, which were adopted in the spring of 2015, which are broadly equivalent to the EU Member States’ obligations. But the EEA Agreement requires that we also establish an obligation to have such legislation, and external monitoring that we follow the obligation. In the light of some of the comments and playoffs in this case, I would like to say something about what this case is not: The EU shall not take over the supervision of Norwegian banks. The financial supervision shall continue to supervise Norwegian banks, insurance companies and other financial undertakings, with the exception of credit rating agencies and trade repositories. As of today, we do not have such undertakings. Nor should the EU system of financial supervision be confused with the banking union with the common crisis management authority, and neither the EU financial supervisory authorities nor the EFTA Surveillance Authority should deal with any future financial crisis in Norway. The procedure for incorporating new EU legislation into the EEA Agreement and implementing it in Norwegian law is not affected by the link with the EU financial supervision system. This concerns issues of full harmonisation, EEA adaptations and the amount and design of legislation. I had more on my heart, but I see that time is up.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-13.ana.ud1289e1324	There is a big and important matter The Storting today will take a decision on. The issue of Norwegian membership of EU financial supervision through the EEA Agreement has been high on the case list for both this and the previous government and has been the subject of demanding negotiations. It is a core area of the EU internal market and of the EEA Agreement. The solution allows the continuation of the EEA Agreement with full participation of Norwegian actors in the EU internal market in the financial sector. The financial crisis has shown that there are good reasons for supranationality in this particular area. By finding a solution that ensures a consistent practice in Europe, we are safeguarding important interests in the Norwegian financial industry and in Norwegian business and households dependent on the Norwegian financial industry. We avoid fragmentation of EEA cooperation for financial services. It is not appropriate for us to place Norwegian financial undertakings directly under EU supervisory authority. The EU side could, for its part, which must be seen in the light of the general experience of the financial crisis, not accept a solution without transnationality where all authority remained at national level in the EFTA countries. The solution was that, in defined situations, the EFTA States give powers to the EFTA Surveillance Authority, ESA, which Norway is affiliated to. This is consistent with the two-pillar structure of the EEA Agreement and compatible with the Constitution. The tasks and powers of ESA are complementary to those of the national supervisory activities. ESA shall exercise its powers as financial supervisory authority – as in other areas – independently of the EFTA States. Before taking a binding decision by ESA, national supervisory authorities shall be invited to assist with technical expertise. The right to make such professional recordings is in addition to and complements the right to participate, without the right to vote, which representatives of the national supervisory authorities of the EEA/EFTA States will be given to work with the three EU supervisory authorities. As stated in the proposal, ESA will not have a legal or other formal obligation to take a decision, or take a decision with a specific content, in the light of the draft by the EU supervisory authorities. This implies that ESA can formally not both fail to take decisions and take a decision with a different content. Any deviation from the decisions taken by the EFTA and EU pillars could be addressed to discussions within the EEA Committee in accordance with the usual rules of the EEA Agreement on dispute resolution. Therefore, the solution does not formally imply a transfer of power to the EU pillar. A fundamental principle is that EEA cooperation should be further developed in the context of the development of the internal market in the EU and in order to ensure legal homogeneity and a level playing field. When the EEA Agreement was concluded, there were almost no agencies and supervision in the EU. Therefore, the participation of the EEA/EFTA States was not regulated in the EEA Agreement. You could say that both the previous government and the current government have had to do new work. Today, EU agencies and supervision are a central element of cooperation on the internal market. This also has a significant impact on EEA cooperation. In order to ensure functioning EEA cooperation, EEA/EFTA countries need to have an adequate link with EU agencies and oversight. Few areas are more important than the financial market area in this way. Norway must have an offensive and solution-oriented approach in order to ensure participation in relevant agencies and supervision, although this may involve some challenges of an institutional or legal nature. Full participation, even without the right to vote, must remain the objective, as has also been the practice. The right to vote for non-Member States in EU agencies and supervision is not applicable. Here, the EU adopts a clear principle approach. The participation of the EEA/EFTA States will be necessary in order to continue to ensure a functioning EEA Agreement, regulatory equality in the EEA area and as much as possible a level playing field for Norwegian enterprises. It is difficult to say exactly about possible wider implications and possible precedents of opening up the transfer of powers in the financial supervision process, in particular because EU agencies and supervision may differ significantly in roles, functions and powers. The Norwegian approach must continue to be that each EEA case must be dealt with on the basis of its own premises and what, according to an overall assessment, serves our best interests and a desire to continue a functioning EEA agreement. It is neither possible nor desirable to find a ‘standard’ solution for the relationship of the EEA/EFTA countries with the European supervisory authorities and agencies. A lot of resources have been invested in this matter, and we believe that the negotiated solution is a good one. It is not realistic that we will be able to achieve better results by resuming negotiations.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-13.ana.ud1289e1354	It's going to be line change.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-13.ana.ud1289e1451	The internal market in the EU has common competition rules applicable to all participating countries. In order to achieve a level playing field in the financial field, the EU also requires EEA countries to be included in the new financial supervision system. This is done by transferring monitoring and action powers to the EFTA Surveillance Authority, ESA, as well as judicial authority to the EFTA Court of Justice, a transfer of powers which causes the government to request the consent of the Stortinge to take place under the Basic Law § 115. We are not moving the Financial Supervisory Board to Brussels. No one has suggested it. Norway will, of course, have its own financial supervision, as before. As at present, Norwegian Financial Supervisory Authority will supervise Norwegian financial institutions and, just as before, Norwegian supervision will be able to impose stricter requirements on Norwegian financial industry than is otherwise seen in Europe. This is about formalising the approach of Norwegian supervision to ESA, as we are part of the internal market through the EEA Agreement. That solution has now been found through an EFTA plan. If we say no, that means that the Norwegian financial industry will be outside the internal market. Norwegian financial industry will have problems assisting Norwegian companies to compete, and Norwegian financial institutions may not sell their savings products in the EU. The EEA is important for Norway. That was the national compromise after we said no to EU membership. The EEA ensures Norwegian business access to our main export market. 75 pst. of our exports go to Europe, and we have a national agricultural and fisheries policy. The model chosen is fully in line with the EEA TPBA. The Norwegian Financial Supervisory Authority shall supervise Norwegian financial institutions. The Norwegian Financial Supervisory Authority shall have its seat in the European Supervisory Authority, without the right to vote. The ESA monitors the compliance of the Norwegian Financial Supervisory Authority with the common regulatory framework, which the Commission monitors the EU side. The EFTA Court judges whether there is disagreement, as the Court of Justice of the European Union does on the EU side. If this model violates the Constitution, the whole EEA Agreement is a breach of the Constitution. It is not that when ESA makes a copy of the European Supervisory Authority, it means that we are subject to the EU. No, there is no legal obligation to make a copy decision, but in an internal market with common legislation, where one supervisory authority monitors 28 Member States and another supervisory authority monitors 3 countries, it would be alarming if the two supervisory authorities were to come out with very different conclusions on the basis of the same legislation. The decision-making authority transferred to the EFTA Surveillance Authority concerns clearly defined cases, mainly cross-border situations where there is a need for mediation between national authorities, crisis situations and the supervision of some key actors, such as the credit rating agency. It is quite reasonable that such powers should be transferred to a supranational level, perhaps also necessary to ensure financial stability in the financial market, where actors operate across national borders. As an EU opponent and an EEA member, I support this change. Norway too needs financial stability in a global world.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-13.ana.ud1289e1464	The financial crisis clearly showed us that the need for a single European regulatory framework was necessary in order to try to avoid new crises. The banking and financial sector is an international sector. Experience shows that the spread effect of crises due to poor regulation and, not least, poor supervision is very fast. The financial and, in particular, the banking sector, but also all other sectors of business, are being affected rapidly. We saw this clearly during the financial crisis, including at home. It was therefore wise for the red-green government to start work on finding a good form of association with the EU’s financial supervision system at the time. Of course, in the light of the results we had seen of the lack of supervision before the crisis, there was no resistance either in the opposition of the Storting or within the Red-Green Government, which set this in motion, and which continued to work in the future. This has been cancelled to the Storting through the annual notifications – in the financial market messages, in budgets, etc. – so that the perception that this case came to the Stortinget, I think it is more because of a lack of interest from Stortinget than information from the changing governments. The size has been regularly informed of the work, now in the sixth year. With this proposition in place, we also have the final formal basis in place to allow our national financial supervision, with the exception of the voting rights, to participate fully and have the possibility to influence the professional judgements made by the supervisory authorities. And if in any area we're just a small country in the world and depending on what happens around us, it's in the financial sector, which is international and global in its orientation, and our isolationism and nationalism gives very poor conditions both for the financial industry and for the control of it. After the crisis, the EU has developed a lot of new legislation on the financial market. Much of this has to do with supervision, and in order to get new legislation into the EEA Agreement, we need a formal solution on how the EFTA countries, among these Norway, should deal with the supervisory system. It is not enough to adapt to EU legislation nationally, as we have done in a number of areas. The EEA Agreement, by its very nature, presupposes that we also establish a formal obligation to have such legislation, and that we are involved in an external monitoring of our compliance with the obligation. This has been a long process, but it has also been thorough, in fact also in the Financial Committee, although many say otherwise, and in this respect: a great tribute to the case-rapporteur, who has done an excellent job. This formal cooperation shall simply try to avoid a new financial crisis. Some of us still remember what it was like in Stortinget when the financial crisis came, how helpless we felt – I put myself in the Financial Committee of the Stortinge at that time. Even our robust economy trembled from the shaking that happened outside of us: the oil price fell, the world's stock exchanges fell, and there was actually nowhere to hide. That's why we're here today discussing technicalities, but also the content of this proposition. Because when you listen to the debate, it's a message that, in my view, is under-communicated here. We talk a lot about what we shouldn't and should not do or participate in, but one question is also: What are we going to contribute to, what are we going to contribute to? That's what this is all about. It is about contributing to financial stability, not only in our own country, but throughout Europe, which also depends on our country. It's about helping to avoid creating new financial crises, which are catching up fast and can also hit our country, hit our business life, hit our economy. And it's not least about -- which we're dealing with in a lot of cases in the financial committee -- helping to avoid a lot of capitalism that we don't want at all. By contributing in this way, we can strengthen not only the competitiveness of the Norwegian financial industry, but the competitiveness of Norwegian business at all. Because that's what's at the bottom of it because we want to connect to these systems in such a way that yes, we can contribute, and yes, we still have decision-making powers, and it will help us and Norwegian business in the long run to get better growth in the Norwegian economy.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-13.ana.ud1289e1562	Nobody wants a new financial crisis. An important measure against this is to establish supervision of national financial supervision to avoid some countries becoming less aware of the requirements for attracting foreign funding. The experience of the previous financial crisis shows that cooperation is an important response to prevent new crises. The international financial crisis showed how bad it can be when banks take more risk than they can bear. Therefore, in the wake of the financial crisis, the EU established a new and more comprehensive system of cooperation on financial supervision. The aim is to make European banks more resilient to crises and bad times. We know that instability from around the world can quickly pass to Norway with its consequences for Norwegian banking customers and our business life. If we refuse to link ourselves to the European System of Financial Supervision, this could mean that Norwegian financial industries will be outside the internal market. This means that the Norwegian financial industry may have difficulties in assisting Norwegian enterprises on the international road, that Norwegian financial institutions, such as the fund companies, cannot sell their savings products in the EU on equal terms, and that we, as foreigners, are likely to be considered more risky by foreign investors. The last one is very serious. Large parts of Norwegian business and a very large proportion of Norwegian housing loans are financed by foreign deposits. In other words, a no could mean more expensive loans for ordinary people and more expensive financing for Norwegian businesses. That's the last thing we need when the Norwegian economy is in the middle of a big shift. If we stay outside European cooperation on financial supervision, we must expect the EU to suspend EEA cooperation on financial services. Financial services are cross-border. It is therefore important that the Norwegian Financial Supervisory Authority has good and close cooperation with its European colleagues. This matter has been dealt with thoroughly over many years. The red-green government, including the centre party, started working in 2009, and the matter has been mentioned and dealt with in the Storting quite 18 times after that.

The EEA Agreement gives us privileged access to the EU's internal market, not least for financial activities. The price to pay for this extensive market access is that the agreement must develop in line with EU regulatory developments and that, of course, post-financial developments in the financial field have been very extensive. The harmonisation of the legislation of the EFTA and EEA countries and the EU Member States is the very premise of the whole EEA regime. So if you have something against the very concept you now have, it's really the EEA agreement you're arguing against. This is a consequence of our EEA membership. So there are a number of important things to do with new supervision – it has to design legislative proposals, and it has to ensure that a uniform manual is set up for the supervision of financial institutions in the internal market, and national self-regulation is negotiated only if we do not comply with these international standards.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-13.ana.ud1289e1933	As rapporteur, I would like to begin by thanking Mr Hansson from the Greens/Greens/European Greens for producing a top-level draft representative. The discovery of the so-called Panama Papers illustrates that. The leak of 11.5 million documents revealed that both state leaders, businessmen, criminals and acquaintances buy secrecy and hide money in tax havens. The scandal not only affects others, it concerns us. Norway's largest bank had been involved and arranged for the Nordics to move money into tax havens that secreted the real owners of large assets. Missing economic openness, capital flow and tax evasion have a number of socially damaging effects. Enormous amounts of money are taxed and thus the public revenue base is clawed out. This weakens the state's ability to solve its tasks and its people's confidence in the authorities and democracy. It's serious enough, but the problem has several sides. Lack of transparency and openness make society more vulnerable to groups involved in drug trafficking, trafficking in human beings, arms trafficking, terrorist financing and other cross-border crime. Document 8:84 S spells out a comprehensive proposal for a decision. It can be divided into two. In the first part, it is proposed that the Storting should ask the government to work actively in cooperation with like-minded countries to build international support for concrete political and legal measures to combat problems related to tax havens and economic secrecy. A consensus committee is behind such a call to the government. The Minister of Finance confirms to the Committee that she subscribes to the problem description and that the government is giving high priority to this work. In the second part of the proposal, the government is asked to initiate a diplomatic process aimed at negotiating a new international convention on economic openness across borders with obligations to comply with a set of minimum rules on financial openness and country-specific corporate reporting. Here the Committee has a more nuanced assessment. The 2009 capital flow selection proposed the establishment of such a convention. The whole committee believes it would be a good solution if such a universal convention were established. But how easily can it be done? The majority think of a strategically important question is which track it is most appropriate to follow in the work to get an impact on greater economic openness. In 2011, the International Law and Policy Institute produced a report on the possible added value of such a convention. The conclusion was that a convention can give added value, but the conclusion remains. Are the countries that have found a niche as a tax haven likely to accede to such a binding convention? Will other approaches in practice provide a better impact? Hopefully, over time will be closed with the establishment of an international commitment to a binding Convention on Financial Transparency. The Committee of My Norwegian Authorities should contribute to such an involvement. A joint committee asks the government to keep the Stortinget informed about the future development of such a convention initiative. As case spokesman, I am happy to have agreed on this, even if there are shades in the view of which track is the most important one to follow. From the Christian People's Party we believe it is possible to work parallel along several tracks. Lack of economic openness has many problematic consequences, and we also need more tools to address the challenge.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-13.ana.ud1289e1950	An international convention on economic openness is a very good idea and will probably be possible at some point in some form. What the case spokesperson went through in a brilliant way here, has great professional support, and over time here in Norway, there are many who have realized that it will be a good idea. The question is whether there is now a political climate that means that Norway should now work directly on it, and my best guess is that what we should do first and foremost now is to end up with the many international initiatives taking place in this area, but with greater force than we have done in the last two years under the government we have. Therefore, first of all, we should continue the work within the OECD with even greater force than today. At that time, we learned that even from those countries that otherwise are very close to us in those questions, there was little interest in that track, just for tactical reasons, that one had more faith in making other progress. ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-13.ana.ud1289e1959	Every year we get new revelations that show that the world has a big and perhaps growing problem of tax evasion, capital evasion and economic crime. Initiatives through the G20 and OECD are important. Initiatives through the EU are coming, but they have proved not to be sufficient to date. That is why the Greens have taken the initiative – the initiative that has emerged in different variants earlier in the UK – to work actively and take an initiative from the Norwegian side to an international convention on economic transparency across national borders with minimum rules on financial transparency and country-specific company reporting. We, who are not in the committee, thank you for a thorough, factual and constructive examination of the proposal and for the statements that have underlined it, from the case-rapporteur and from the representative of Solhjell. We note that the Committee concludes that Norway should continue to keep its priority in the international forums where work is already under way on tax evasion and capital evasion and on financial transparency. The Committee points out, however, that it is natural that Norwegian authorities also contribute to the promotion of an international convention, and asks the government to keep the Stortinget informed of the future development of the possibilities for such an initiative. One way to strengthen this work would be for Norway itself to show the way and establish stricter minimum rules on financial transparency and country-specific corporate reporting for its own funds, for Norwegian companies and individuals and international companies operating in Norway. The main argument against a convention, namely that it will be difficult to obtain the consent of countries that deliberately contribute to capital flight and tax evasion, is a kind of argument that applies to all initiatives to create international cooperation on something which is initially major and widespread international problems. Similarly, those actors with an interest in preventing greater transparency in financial transactions are not open to join, for example. OECD initiative. It is quite right that there has been a lot of financial secrecy in recent years, but on the other hand, as the leaks of the last few months have shown, we are very far from being in the goal. The question probably has to be whether we are moving in the right direction at all. An international convention is not intended to replace the initiatives already under way, but as a superstructure and a strengthening and coordination of these initiatives. The OECD CRS initiative, The Common Reporting Standard, is positive and useful, but the problems posed by international financial environments go far beyond tax evasion and aggressive tax planning. There are many sides that are not covered in this type of agreement. Bank boxes are not included. Release ports such as airports where high values are stored are not included. The agreement is clearly lacking in enforcement, such as dispute resolution mechanisms, etc. If a country such as Norway is to be able to both pursue its own interests and to contribute, in particular, to help developing developing developing countries to reduce capital mobility, it needs capacity building and greater international cooperation. The OECD CRS initiative does not contain this either. I therefore take note of the counter-representation to which the representative, Solhjell, referred, inter alia. strategic, towards taking an initiative that we propose today. I do not doubt the good intentions behind that kind of strategic thinking, either from Norwegian actors or from foreign countries, but we must note that this is a type of issue where restraint has at least partly the consequence that the problem continues and is not resolved. This also leads to a demonstration in Norway of the existence, even in Norwegian financial systems, of a culture and a view that, above all, smart behaviour with money in an internationally unclear system is ok. Therefore, it is important that the Storting and the Government send a signal that the tolerance for that type of activity is lower than before. It is important that it is understood that there is a willingness to do more than before, and it is important that it becomes visible that there is a will for a more forward-looking attitude than before. That is why we promoted this proposal. We are pleased that it has received so much positive reception, and we encourage the strongest government to step up the activity of internationally unfortunate operations in financial environments, and to continue to look at a convention as one of the possibilities.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-13.ana.ud1289e2018	It's been 17 years since the Stortinget dealt with a message from the government about our energy system. So even I was only 13 years old when the energy policy was thoroughly debated in this Chamber. Since 1999 and the previous energy message, the mobile phone has become all-rounder, our houses have received more and more advanced electrical appliances while they have become more energy efficient. Since 2000, 84 and 47 gigawatts of wind and solar power have been built in Europe, respectively. Coal, oil and nuclear power have all gone back. We've got Leaf and Tesla, the first electric cars to reach large groups of buyers. Thus, there have been major changes in the energy field since the last report to the European Council on energy policy, both in Norway and in the world. At the same time, the fight against climate change has been given a growing place in our awareness and is taking the lead in the policy that will prevail in the years to come. Not least, the Paris Agreement, which we are going to discuss here in this Chamber tomorrow, places considerable weight on Norwegian energy and climate policy. Everyone is talking about the green shift, but it is when you see security of supply, climate and economic development in the context that the concept has concrete content. The green shift in Norway is not primarily about a switch from coal to gas or renewable in the power sector, as it does in many other countries in Europe and the world. 99 pst. of all power generation in Norway comes from hydropower. In Norway, therefore, the green shift is more about phasing out fossil use in other sectors and using our fantastic hydropower in new areas. We often hear that Norway is built on the wealth of oil and our gas, but it is really the hydropower that has been, and is, the carrier of modern Norway was built and industrialized on. This good supply of renewable and non-emission power puts Norway in a unique position to further develop an industry with low emissions and phase-out fossil consumption in other sectors. This has also been my first experience as rapporteur for a big message. It has been a demanding work, also because the Committee on Energy and the Environment has dealt with a large number of issues this spring. I would like to congratulate the members of the Committee on the good work that has been done and on the fact that the Storting can today deal with a position containing a broad majority on many important road choices. Then I will not hide that there has also been frustration about short deadlines and such things as hearing with a large work pressure. I interpret that as a strong desire of all parties to have a hand on the wheel of the process. It has been important for the government parties to seek a majority first with our cooperation parties the Christian People's Party and the Left, which is completely in line with our cooperation agreement. This may not have been a big surprise for anyone, but I am nevertheless pleased that the committee has finally issued an opinion that is unanimous on many points. The impact notification and energy policy focus primarily on how to ensure a stable supply of electricity. Power is the basic and critical infrastructure on which we are all totally dependent, and on which our modern society stops without it. Therefore, there should be no need for the notification to focus on the framework conditions for the production and distribution of electrical energy. Energy policy has also been given a different and larger dimension, which is about climate and the environment. This is the theme highlighted in the Communication, which we have raised in the Committee's discussion. The result is a holistic approach that addresses security of supply, renewable energy production, framework conditions, issues of protection and use, and not least the climate aspect of energy use. Norway is an energy nation. The hydropower is and will be the backbone of our energy supply and is a competitive advantage for Norwegian business. It is our duty to manage the resource that renewable energy represents in the best possible way. I am therefore pleased that it is a unanimous committee that adheres to the principle of turning energy policy from subsidising the production of known renewable energy to supporting the development of new climate and environmental technologies. This is important to ensure that Norwegian renewable energy is profitable, but it is also important because Norway’s perhaps main contribution to the global reduction of greenhouse gases will be the very development of new technologies. In practice, this means that the Stortinget will today decide not to continue the electrical certification system for Norway, and we are giving leadership and signals on Enova’s new mandate. This also means that the Committee endorses the government's proposals for simplification in concession proceedings, and the majority will also wind up the overall plan. Renewable energy must be profitable in order for it to be sustainable. If we are to succeed in the transition to the low-emission society, it must pay off to make environmentally friendly choices. The notification of the Government and the decision of the majority are based on the objective of decoupling economic growth from emissions. The attitude has been clearly green, and the majority has a wide range of proposals on how to use renewable energy more fully to phase out fossil energy in all sectors. If we're going to do this in a good way, it requires good knowledge. I am therefore pleased that the bourgeois parties agreed at an early stage that the government should already, at the time of the government budget, make this harvest visible and mention emission reduction targets in the non-quota sector. This work presupposes the submission of the EU Commission’s effort-sharing proposal and underlines the close link between Norwegian climate policy and the EU. In particular, the transport sector, which accounts for the largest domestic fossil energy use of around 54 TWh in 2014. There can be no doubt that phasing out fossil energy for transport is becoming very important in order to achieve the emission targets we have set ourselves. Therefore, the bourgeois parties proposed that the Government of the National Transport Plan 2018-2029 should set targets for the number of low- and zero-emission vehicles in 2025. These target figures shall follow agreed climate objectives and the potential proposed by the disciplines. Let me also quickly add that the setting contains a number of proposals that facilitate the use of hydrogen to a greater extent, higher ambitions for biofuels and green shipping bets. Enova is already one of the most important tools we have in climate policy. This is further reinforced in the Communication and in the decision of the majority here today. Enova’s economic muscles need to be strengthened in the coming years and it is absolutely necessary for them to be able to cope with the tasks now assigned to them. I am not about to advance future budgetary processes, but rather to raise the majority's point of view on a new principle of financing Enova. In view of the phase-out of that el-certificate scheme, the majority would like to see consumers' obligations relating to this scheme met in the network tariff. As a power customer, this should not be particularly marked on the bill, so there is no new charge in addition to what consumers are already charged. Such income will enable Enova to support projects both in industry, in the transport sector, in construction and new innovative climate and environmental technologies. 17 years is a long time, and progress is fast. What do I know, perhaps the case rapporteur is going for a future energy report in high school today. What I know is that she or he will be part of a society where energy is produced, distributed and used in ways that we haven't even thought about today. It will be a society where greenhouse gas emissions are significantly reduced or completely lost. Hopefully, we've been there today to create the basis for such a development. I look forward to the debate.

Enova needs to suggest that we reduce greenhouse gas emissions, we need to strengthen security of energy supply, and we need to develop climate and environmental technologies. It is also important that we focus on the continued development of local energy production. This is important for the green shift, to get people on the team to develop technology. In this message, we are adding up to continue to bet on solar energy. Much of the Committee's attitude is about transport. It is important that we reach the target for the number of low- and zero-emission vehicles, including passenger cars, vans, buses and heavy-duty vehicles, by 2030. This is historical, and international attention has also been given. Only a few months ago, it was primarily Left and some others who talked about it being possible for all new cars to be zero-emission cars in 2025. I have even received criticism of leadership in some of the country's most important newspapers to just present this goal. Today I expect that it will be unanimous or almost unanimous that the Storting is in favour of such a goal. It's a big step in the right direction. Because we have to have so high ambitions to achieve the targets of 40 pst. greenhouse gas emission saving up to 2030. After 2025 new private cars, city buses and light goods vehicles shall be a zero-emission vehicle. By 2030 new heavy goods vehicles, 75 pst. of new long-distance buses and 50 pst. of new trucks: zero-emission vehicles. In 2050, transport shall be nearly emission-free. This means that we have to keep the benefits of a zero-emission vehicle today. We have to keep them so long that we actually achieve the goal. We need to use the entire tax system to make pollution more expensive, while at the same time buying the environmentally friendly options, and we need to build an infrastructure for zero-emission vehicles across the country. Enova will be essential to achieve this. Biofuels are also important in the setting up of the Committee.
