It's an ambitious goal, and the first step in this is, of course, to get more hydrogen filling stations – which can cover the whole country. The first stations will enter the order for Enova, so these can be put in place as early as next year. We make an order against the National Transport Plan to look at the possibilities within the Norwegian railway system where we do not currently have electrified operations. In the Norwegian industry, in the quota sector, much needs to be done in the coming years, both because of the need to reduce emissions, in order for Norwegian industry to retain its status as a leading climate-friendly business, and because we are to develop new technologies that can help reduce global emissions. Carbon capture and storage are important and become even more important in the decades ahead with the Paris Agreement's ambition to reach a 1.5 degree target. This means that we have to have negative emissions at an international level in the past. This means that carbon capture and storage will need a completely different pressure over the years to come, and Norway will have to lead in this process. The government is asked to set a target of 10 TWh of reduced energy use in existing buildings, compared to the current level. This will release energy that can replace fossil energy. It's a very risky signal, and it has to be corrected. The majority in Stortinget therefore still believe that Norwegian oil policy has nothing to do with Norwegian energy policy. The fossil comrades from the Progress Party, the Labour Party and the Right, and the Center Party, with its varying support players, maintain that this is good climate policy, for example. adding 900 million tonnes of extra CO2 to the earth's atmosphere, which is what will come out of burning up the oil from the Johan Sverdrup field, and perhaps even more hundreds of millions of tonnes from new fields in the 23rd concession round, only by power from land. As long as this intrusive real-life denial underpins Norwegian energy and climate policy, Norway will labor to do its part of the climate work and labor to get us through a green shift, which is a prerequisite for well-being and a secure future. The message describes, as many have pointed out — and I think that is natural in such a message — the fact that we began to reap from our fantastic hydropower resources more than 100 years ago and created the basis for the industrialisation of Norway and the development of the welfare state. We have also tried to describe how the energy management and the principles of energy management have served us all very well, given that we have developed a starting point where the energy resources are to be harvested and managed in a way that benefits the entire Norwegian people. Both the power management and later the oil and gas management have served us all very well. We have also tried in the Energy Communication to describe how the countries around us now face major challenges in their power system with large impacts of animals and controversy – at least in many countries – nuclear power and large impacts of polluting coal power, which are both controversial and significantly contributing to greenhouse gas emissions, and how many countries face increasing challenges with the integration of new renewable, non-regulatable power, such as wind and sun, while in Norway we have a completely different and very good starting point for the future. We have large renewable energy resources in the form of the hydropower we have developed and the hydropower we can develop in the future, but also in the form of wind, both along the coast and in large parts of the inland. We have an energy supply that, despite being a large country with a relatively pig-grown population, is efficient and safe and has very low greenhouse gas emissions. It is often forgotten that if we had replaced our power supply with a power supply that had a very similar energy mix to the energy mix in Europe, the electricity supply alone in Norway would have represented one and a half times the total Norwegian greenhouse gas emissions, but it does not, because it is based on renewable energy sources. We also use this fantastic renewable energy for much more than most of our neighbouring countries, not only for heat in our houses, but to a greater and greater extent and more than any other country, also for mobility and transport. We have built a good transmission system in this large country, which has strengthened the efforts of this government. We will soon have all our parts of the country connected to a well-functioning 420 kV network, and we are – and that is important today, and will become more important in the future – early on to take up new technological opportunities. This is a good starting point for important energy and climate work in the time ahead. We haven't come here by ourselves, and it's not going to be maintained by itself in the future either. That is why we have presented this Communication, where security of supply, climate and value creation are seen in the context. The renewal surplus has increased, with the opportunities it offers, but it has also challenged the profitability of upgrading existing infrastructure. Norwegian power generation is currently close to 100 pst. renewable. We can thank a wide range of forward-looking politicians and industrialists for that. It was not climate policy considerations that led to the role of hydropower in Norway, but at a time when global warming is defined as our main challenge, we can see that Norwegian society is in a special position, thanks to a sometimes separable power policy over time. Today we have a positive force balance in normal years. The next thing we had to deal with was a report that was ordered by the previous government. The Reiten Committee presented its report ‘A better organised electricity network’ in spring 2014. The report gave us advice on organizing the web for a better future. There was a need for changes to enable us to build the renewable society. Today's organization needed to be tightened up. And there was a need for changes in the Energy Act, actually to finalise the changes that were already begun in 1991. This work started immediately and ended with the matter we had to deal with in Stortinget just before Easter, about changes in the Energy Act. We have put in place the changes that are necessary for us to have a network that is organised in the best possible way and is able to meet customers and consumers who are not only to receive electricity, but could also be net suppliers in the network. In addition, we are revoking the Energy Act so that other people than Statnet can expand cables abroad. I am not going to forge a legislative change, but it is a wish that private people can get on the road with Statnet, so that more creative players can create more green jobs in Norway. The next thing that lies in the foundation of a renewable future is that we need to get applications for enlargement through the bureaucracy and simplify the procedure. We've given a lot of concessions, simplified the procedure, and started to develop, so that those who want to expand will reach the 2021 deadline for obtaining certificates. Renewal needs to be facilitated at the same time as we build up the grid, i.e. transmission capacity. It's not useful to be allowed to build up new power if you don't reach the market with the power. That is why we have focused heavily on expanding and strengthening the network in Norway. Many concessions have been granted, and many major projects are under construction. New transmission network in Lofoten is in place, and a concession has also been granted for the construction of Bjerkvik-Skaidi. I mention these for the simple reason that there are two major projects that are important to my part of the country. But there are many other major expansions going on, and it's added to an investment only in the 50 billion transmission network. kr for the next 10 years. Ditto is to be invested in distribution networks and we need to be able to deploy charging capacity for electric cars or facilitate shore-side electricity in our ports. In addition, we need to provide green jobs. Work is ongoing and, until the autumn, the government is presenting its work on green competitiveness and new industrial notification. But we don't sit and wait, and the energy message is the last hand at the work, along with amendments to the concession law, where we introduce an ANS and a DA model to ensure predictable power for the industry. We provide for the development of power in sheltered watercourses to prevent floods and escapes. We continue to simplify by removing the total plan for hydropower expansion, and we're making a plan for wind power construction. There are raised doubts about the guarantees of origin, which we have parked, in the same way that we maintain the municipality's right to retain the concession power and the concession fee. We establish that the backbone of our renewable society is hydropower. We are opening up renewable projects related to sea wind by proposing the realisation of a demonstration project. We liquidate the green certificates and signal that we do not want to subsidize known technology for the future, but will also use subsidies for new technologies and opportunities.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-13.ana.ud1289e3193	As the rapporteur, I would like to thank the Committee for its good cooperation on this matter. There are important questions about this issue, and it is clearly a policy issue that is important and more topical than ever. The proposers point to low wages, overtime pressures, poor safety and deficiencies in several areas affecting large parts of the world’s product production. Norwegian consumers have little information and few rights in terms of transparency and knowledge of how the product has been produced. The proposers believe that there should be a law on transparency and information on how companies deal with societal responsibilities and supply chains, i.e. a law requiring the enterprise to respond to consumers and organisations on issues related to their efforts to improve production conditions. The aim must be to provide consumers with information on ethical aspects relating to the products. The scope of application of the law should be those of, inter alia: textile, clothing, shoes, electronics, ICT, furniture, coffee, tea, cocoa and toys. In the opinion of the proposers, the law should apply to all companies registered in Norway, i.e. all companies importing goods into Norway and at the same time being a legal entity in Norway. The law should, in the view of the proposers, be enforced by a complainant. An overall committee believes that transparency is an important principle, and only by transparency will it be possible to identify how companies produce their goods. In order to ensure that national and international companies do not violate human rights, it is possible to verify what is happening in several stages of production, without this implying that the company must state its business secrets. We, as politicians, are engaged in contributing to increased transparency and increased access to information on the ethical aspects of the production of goods and services. If the ethical information requirement is to have a real effect on products and consumers, the transparency requirements must apply throughout the production chain and not only back one link. The information must entail the fulfilment of a minimum requirement. In its statement, a joint committee refers to the United Nations' guiding principles for business. It underlines that industry has a separate responsibility to protect human rights, including fundamental professional rights in countries in which it operates. It implies that companies must have their own human rights procedures in place where there is a risk of breach of such rights. It is important to point out that several labelling schemes require the ethical guidelines of companies, such as purely ethical labels such as Fairtrade. Official eco-labels such as the Nordic Swan Label and the European EU-Ecolabel also impose ethical requirements as well as stringent environmental requirements. Unfortunately, it seems that manufacturers may find it problematic to publish their own supply chain in terms of competitive conditions. The eco-labels and ethics labels treat this type of competitively sensitive information confidentially and can therefore be good instruments for manufacturers who want to make their social and environmental involvement visible without full transparency about their entire supply chain. Targeted use of eco-labels and ethics on products classified as high-risk products by Diffi, such as textiles, clothing, shoes, etc., in combination with promotional information to consumers, will meet this need of the legislative proposal, in which case the need for manufacturers to protect competitively sensitive information will be addressed. Consumers receive information at the very moment of purchase and will opt out of products and goods without labelling. As a politician, I believe it is important to make market forces work for the best of ethics and the environment.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-13.ana.ud1289e3219	Norway can lead the way in many areas, and the introduction of a possible ethical information law is absolutely a front line in a policy area that creates a high level of consumer involvement in Norway as well as in the rest of the world. The big question is where, if necessary, the restrictions in the law will go. In this way, the working party is positive to an investigation that will allow for the limitations of an ethics information law, if any. Today, ethics information is sought by many. It is a great commitment, and the organisations that are currently working to promote good ethics in Norway are meeting with a lot of consumers. The challenge is that today, suppliers are not obliged to provide that information. One can develop a company that takes ethical considerations and makes good, conscious choices. It is possible to run the business well over time and have an advantage of providing ethics information. Yes, it can be extra profitable – according to business-economic ideas, even – if you have a conscious relationship to this. Stormberg is a company that is an example of how you want to publish this kind of information on your own. The time has come to give full transparency and to us as consumers the right to a certain amount of information – both in terms of production itself and in terms of how the producers work on ethical trade, and in terms of the requirements they place on their supply chain. Bad employment should not be a competitive advantage.

We point to some clarification needs and solutions in the proposal for a representative, and we point to the Environmental Information Act, which we know has an important function. In order for the market and consumer power to function, consumers need to know what they buy and that they do not contribute to unethical production conditions in their trade. The Committee considers that there is a need for an investigation and that good assessments are made as to whether or not such a law should be introduced.

In many parts of the world, human rights are broken every day by the production of goods sold to us in Norway. Everything from clothing and mobile phones to coffee, cocoa and tobacco can be produced in companies where people are exposed to health damage or exploited in slave labor or child labour. We don't have to turn the clock back more than 100 years to find unfathomable suffering and death in our own working life. For many toothpick workers at the end of the 1800s, dental work was a first sign that the jawbone gradually rotted away. Serious brain damage and death could eventually be the price to pay for the work on the dangerous substance yellow phosphorus. In 1889, the female feminists demonstrated in Kristiana. They organized themselves, had real strike management and collected into the strike box. They lost their first battle, but they had fulfilled their demands step by step. The fight they started has given us a Norway today with protection agents, trade unions and labour inspectorates. Now the fight against unfair employment for low-cost textile workers has reached the western clothing markets, because our lives are linked to theirs. The laborer's bloodshed hangs around our necks. In the back of many of our clothes, it says «Made in Bangladesh». There were 29 Western companies that got their clothes produced in the Rana Plaza building, which collapsed. When the evening post through its award-winning series Sweatshop allows Norwegian youth to try themselves as textile workers in Cambodia, the tears come from the young, who understand what kind of life the factory workers have. In low-cost countries like China, Bangladesh and Cambodia, it is, to say the least, not always as easy to be a trade union activist. They deserve our support. Consumer campaigns have often led to change. Under pressure from consumers, several clothes chains have chosen to open up where they produce their clothes. Nevertheless, there is a long way to go from openness to justice. We're going to demand that the textile workers get better relationships, but most importantly, they have the opportunity and the belief that it's useful to raise the claim themselves. They must stand together and have the strength to not enter buildings that have cracks in the wall. They need their own voice and cohesion so that they can require regulated working hours and protection against dangerous conditions. But if we're going to be able to listen to their voices, we're dependent on the sound carrying all the way forward. There's much more meaning in asking questions about the workers who sew our clothes, getting organized, if it's actually possible to get an answer. SV has proposed to the Storting that when the State and the municipalities purchase goods, fundamental rights shall be required to be fulfilled. Prohibition of discrimination, prohibition of the worst forms of child labour, prohibition of forced labour, and not least the right to organise and conduct collective bargaining are important international conventions on labour standards which have been accepted by most countries. What will be the consequence of setting basic ethical standards for the goods you purchase? – Well, there's going to be a competition at something more than just a price. I hope that will also be the case when you individually make your choices, but with a knowledge you may not have had before. We have to remember that someone pays the price – someone is doing it today with life and health. That is why an ethics law is needed, and SV promises to be in the fight all the way forward.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-13.ana.ud1289e3324	Too many people produce goods to the world market under inhumane conditions. In addition, this replacement of people very often goes hand in hand with the replacement of nature. Product production that does not pay for the human costs or environmental costs of production is the fuel itself in a global consumption gap that is both ethical and environmentally unsustainable. It is therefore necessary to ensure responsible production and consumption of one of the UN's recently adopted sustainability targets, by which Norway is also obliged. Ensuring ethically sound working conditions for workers around the world is not only a natural moral commitment, it is also an important contribution to social and political stability, and it is strongly linked to a more responsible attitude towards the environment. Therefore, it is both reasonable and also in our own interest to secure these relationships. Norwegian consumers today receive little information and have few rights to transparency and knowledge about how the goods we buy are produced. We may have a legitimate suspicion that the pants we buy at NOK 198 are based on the replacement of workers, but we do not know for sure. We also don't know if the neighboring pants of NOK 2,500 are produced in the same bad way. Therefore, it is a joyous progress that the Stortinget today asks the government to enforce an ethics information law and consider proposing such a law. In a survey that several have already referred to from the pulpit here, over 70 pst. of those who asked that they were willing to pay more to ensure acceptable remuneration and employment of producers. This development is a victory for consumer power, responsible Norwegian companies and solidarity workers, who have pressed for increased transparency about how goods are produced. Organisations such as the Future in our hands, the Rain Forest Fund, WWF and others have contributed in various ways to attention on how clothing is produced, how timber is produced, how natural products, animal products, etc. delivered to us. All of this contributes to a proper development. Ethical trade initiatives support the intention of an Ethics Information Act, and a number of enterprises, organisations and public enterprises want legal requirements for the transparency of workers producing goods imported into Norway. Large Norwegian import companies, as well as employers' organisations such as Works, NHO Handel and KS, agree with LO, YS, Delta, Parat and the Joint Association on the trade union side. The Greens therefore expect the government to react quickly and come back to Stortinget with an investigation and hopefully a concrete bill. An ethical information law must aim to increase the transparency of the working conditions of production companies and to contribute to measures to improve the conditions of workers. Today, companies themselves decide how much information they want to share with us consumers. Some share much, others share little, but equal demands for transparency give consumers the power to promote the good and punish the bad importers and producers. When buying a T-shirt, the trade mark should be required by law to provide information on the working conditions of the production company. For the consumer, it will then be easier to make choices that are ethically right for herself, while at the same time contributing to better labour rights in producer countries. Information on verifiable products and working conditions increases the importer’s interest in better manufacturing conditions with their suppliers. Such a law would confer a competitive advantage on undertakings selling goods which have been produced in a responsible manner. An Ethics Information Act should apply to all establishments in Norway, including those selling not only consumer goods, but for example. industry inputs. Finally, I would like to thank the Christian People's Party and the Centre for their good cooperation on a good and important proposal. An overall family and culture committee is behind examining the legal requirement for transparency for workers producing goods imported into Norway. We are looking forward to the government coming back to Stortinget about this. I recommend the Committee's attitude.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-13.ana.ud1289e3341	As Minister of Consumer Affairs, I share the view of the Committee that consumers should have a real opportunity to make informed choices when they act. Access to information on environmental and working conditions in the supply chain will improve the choice of consumers on the basis of their own preferences. We have seen examples that consumer requirements can push companies to be more open about their own supply chains. However, it is unclear whether making such information available to Norwegian consumers alone will contribute to better manufacturing conditions in other parts of the world, as proposers seem to believe. Despite the fact that seven out of ten consumers state that they will pay more to ensure fair purchases, it turns out that this is not as easily followed up in the day-to-day choices. The proposal to seek out an ethics information law is primarily linked to consumers’ right to information on where and how the goods they purchase are made. In view of this, I think it would be useful to examine the proposal in more detail. Nevertheless, I would like to point out that the main challenge, as I see it, is not primarily access to consumer-oriented information. The problem is that for few consumers actively demand this information in which they act. As it is today, the largest Norwegian companies are already obliged by the Accounting Act § 3-3(c) to publish annual reports on their own work on social responsibility. In addition to this, traders are free to make their own work visible and to help consumers make informed choices. I perceive that many businessmen are already concerned about this. They like to share information about production sites and their work on social responsibility and the environment with consumers and other stakeholders. Ethical trade initiatives have an important role to play here. This is multi-party business cooperation to promote systematic work on social responsibility in the supply chain. The 160 members of the Norwegian business community and public agencies commit themselves both to work with social responsibility in their own supply chain and to make annual reports on this work available on the organisation’s websites. In many industries, companies compete to hijack more and more conscious and socially connected customers. The use of labelling schemes, the transparency of suppliers and thorough work on social responsibility in its own supply chain provide a competitive advantage in the struggle for the customers concerned with ethical and environmental issues. Several labelling schemes provide verifiable, consumer-oriented information on supply chain conditions. The Eco-label Swan may be the best-known official label providing this type of information to consumers. An increasing number of establishments see the value of using the Swan to inform them that strict requirements have been imposed on the production of their goods. It is important to encourage all traders to both maintain and make their work visible for ethics in the supply chain. In October 2015, the government launched an action plan on business and human rights. Here we address clear expectations for business to be careful and to identify risks related to the rights of people and workers of their own suppliers. The action plan clarifies the government’s expectations as to how entrepreneurs can discharge social responsibility in their supply chains. When Stortinget now asks the government to investigate a law on ethics information, I will start that work as soon as possible, and keep Stortinget properly informed. I would like to stress that the government wants to simplify legislation and reporting requirements for business. It will be important for the investigation to respond to whether new legislation is the way to go in this area. It is also clear that an ethics information law, among other things. will need to be assessed against commercial policy and competition considerations and obligations.

To the Christian People's Party's family policy: Why is family policy so important? The Christian People's Party believes it is right to prioritize schemes that ensure safe growth for children and a good framework for families. It is primarily about children needing someone around them to take care of them. It is essential for them to evolve and for them to have a good life — as a child, and later as young people and as adults. For the Christian People's Party, the goal of family policy is, above all, the best possible growth for all children, and we believe that a good framework for families is among the most important means of achieving this. Family policies can also contribute to social cohesion and contribute to gender equality. But the main purpose is safe growth and strong families. It is important for the Christian People's Party to warn against a family policy designed primarily to ensure participation in working life. We agree that high participation in working life is important, but family policy is primarily about the needs of children and the needs of families. Good family policies enable us to give birth to children in Norway and ensure that children grow up safely. It supports around the families and ensures that both parents can combine family and work life, if they so wish. The Christian People's Party wants parents to have more time with their children, not less. It is an important premise for family policy that families are different and have different needs. The primary goal of the Christian People's Party is a child-friendly society, a society where children's needs are prioritised and given priority. Because children are different and have different needs, it is important that family policies enable families to make different choices, and not all families to choose exactly the same solutions. In conclusion, I would like to point out the good comments in the attitude on family protection and on how the Stortinget takes care of the further development of family protection and of the important partners of family protection, including Alternatives to violence, who offer a specialist service that complements the provision of family protection. Family protection services have been strongly strengthened in recent years. Together with the Left and Christian People's Party, the government has put in place a historic commitment to family protection. Over the last three years, the budget has increased by over 90 million. kr. This has resulted in an increase of more than 60 years of service. The increase will strengthen treatment capacity, reduce waiting time and increase the scope for prevention and cooperation with other family-oriented services. In order to ensure greater and more robust professional environments, family protection services have undergone a process where small offices have been merged. The provision of the population has not been affected by this. The need for advice, treatment and mediation is addressed by out-of-office and branch offices. The total number of locations is similar to before the aggregations. Parental support is provided by municipalities, non-profit institutions and through the child and family protection services. The vast majority of parents and families have questions about education and demarcation from time to time. A digital and knowledge-based counselling service aimed at both families with young and older children will be established. Key priorities in family protection in 2016 are work on high-conflict cases, with families living with violence, and parents who experience child protection taking care of their children. To meet these challenges, there are established centres of excellence. They shall disseminate good competence and knowledge of practices, and that is an important work. The notification refers to equivalent parenthood both before, during and after cohabitation and discusses the provisions of the Children’s Act on parental responsibility, residence and association. These proposals take care of all families, both without and with problems.
